=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer n which represents an array nums containing the numbers from 1 to n in order. Additionally, you are given a 2D array conflictingPairs, where conflictingPairs[i] = [a, b] indicates that a and b form a conflicting pair.
Remove exactly one element from conflictingPairs. Afterward, count the number of non-empty subarrays of nums which do not contain both a and b for any remaining conflicting pair [a, b].
Return the maximum number of subarrays possible after removing exactly one conflicting pair.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 4, conflictingPairs = [[2,3],[1,4]]
Output: 9
Explanation:

Remove [2, 3] from conflictingPairs. Now, conflictingPairs = [[1, 4]].
There are 9 subarrays in nums where [1, 4] do not appear together. They are [1], [2], [3], [4], [1, 2], [2, 3], [3, 4], [1, 2, 3] and [2, 3, 4].
The maximum number of subarrays we can achieve after removing one element from conflictingPairs is 9.


Example 2:

Input: n = 5, conflictingPairs = [[1,2],[2,5],[3,5]]
Output: 12
Explanation:

Remove [1, 2] from conflictingPairs. Now, conflictingPairs = [[2, 5], [3, 5]].
There are 12 subarrays in nums where [2, 5] and [3, 5] do not appear together.
The maximum number of subarrays we can achieve after removing one element from conflictingPairs is 12.


 
Constraints:

2 <= n <= 10^5
1 <= conflictingPairs.length <= 2 * n
conflictingPairs[i].length == 2
1 <= conflictingPairs[i][j] <= n
conflictingPairs[i][0] != conflictingPairs[i][1]

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxSubarrays(self, n: int, conflictingPairs: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Definitions:
For each occurrence p=(a,b):
L_low(p) = max { a_q | q occurrence with b_q < b }. If none 0.
E_b(p) = max { a_q | q occurrence with b_q = b, q != p }. If none 0.
Then affected l: L_p < l <= a_p, where L_p = max(L_low, E_b). Need prove with duplicates. Condition no lower b edge with a_q >= l: l > max a_q for lower. yes. Condition no equal b other with a_q >= l: l > max a_q among equal excluding p. yes. Then p included if l <= a_p. Good. If L_p may be >= a_p, interval empty.

Compute L_low for each occurrence: maximum a among all occurrences with b less than current b. Since b values 1..n. Compute E_b for each occurrence: maximum a among other occurrences with same b. For each b group, if group size =1, E_b=0. If group size >=2, for each occurrence with a, E_b = max a among group excluding itself. This is:
- If occurrence's a is not equal to group max? Then E_b = group max.
- If occurrence's a equals group max, E_b = second max a in group (could equal max if duplicate same max; if duplicate same max, second max = max, so E_b=max). More generally for each occurrence, E_b = max_a_group if there is another occurrence with that max; else second_max. Need handle duplicates.
Simpler: For each b group, collect a values with counts. For each occurrence, E_b = max a in group excluding one occurrence. If count of max_a >=2, E_b = max_a. Else E_b = second_max_a (or 0). if a == max1 and count_max1 ==1: E_b = max2
else E_b = max1
If count_max1>=2, even if a==max1, excluding one leaves another max, so max1. If a<max1, excluding it leaves max1. Good. If group size=1, max2=0, a==max1 count1 => E_b=0. Good.

- For each occurrence in group, L_low = max_a_less (max a among all groups with smaller b). But if group has multiple occurrences, same L_low.

- After processing group, update max_a_less = max(max_a_less, max a in group). Need max a in group.

Let's derive from original problem:
For fixed set of forbidden pairs E, valid subarrays count = sum_l (min_b(l)-l), where min_b(l)=min{b | (a,b) in E, a>=l}, or n+1. Because for each l, the smallest right endpoint b among forbidden pairs whose left endpoint is at least l. If r >= b, then subarray [l,r] contains that pair; if r < b, no pair can be fully contained? Need prove: Suppose there is a forbidden pair (a,b) with l<=a and b<=r. Then b is among set, so min_b <= b <= r. Thus if r >= min_b, invalid. Conversely if r >= min_b, choose edge attaining min_b with a>=l and b=min_b<=r, so invalid. Thus valid r are l..min_b-1. Since min_b > l? Need if min_b can be <=l? Edge has a<b and a>=l, so b>a>=l, so min_b>l. Good. Count min_b-l. If no edge, min_b=n+1. Good.

Now removal effect:
For each occurrence p=(a,b), p is the unique edge that attains min_b(l) iff:
- a>=l (p included)
- no q with b_q < b and a_q>=l
- no q with b_q = b and a_q>=l, q != p (unique)
Let L_low = max a_q over q with b_q < b. If l > L_low, no lower q has a_q>=l. If l <= L_low, there is lower q included, so p not min. For equal: E_b = max a_q over q with b_q=b, q!=p. If l > E_b, no equal other included. If l <= E_b, equal other included, p not unique (or if same b, min still b but not unique). Thus p unique min exactly for max(L_low,E_b) < l <= a_p. Good.

When p unique min, original min_b(l)=b. After removing p, new min_b(l) = min b_q over q != p, a_q>=l. Since p was unique min, this is the second smallest b among all occurrences with a>=l. Let M2(l). So gain = sum over l in interval (M2(l)-b). Great.

Implementation plan:

class Solution:
    def maxSubarrays(self, n: int, conflictingPairs: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m = len(conflictingPairs)
        starts = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]  # start -> list of end b
        by_b = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]    # end b -> list of start a
        occ_a = []
        occ_b = []
        for x,y in conflictingPairs:
            if x < y:
                a,b = x,y
            else:
                a,b = y,x
            # a != b guaranteed, but if x<y; if not, swap
            starts[a].append(b)
            by_b[b].append(a)
            occ_a.append(a)
            occ_b.append(b)
        m = len(occ_a)

        # compute L_low and E_b
        L_low = [0]*m
        E_b = [0]*m
        max_a_less = 0
        # Need map from occurrence to group? We can iterate b from1..n and by_b[b] contains a's in input order. Need assign L_low for each occurrence. But by_b lists don't store occurrence index. We can store tuples (a, idx) in by_b. Let's do by_b[b].append((a, idx)).
        by_b = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
        starts = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
        occ_a=[]; occ_b=[]
        for idx,(x,y) in enumerate(conflictingPairs):
            if x<y: a,b=x,y
            else: a,b=y,x
            starts[a].append(b)
            by_b[b].append((a, idx))
            occ_a.append(a); occ_b.append(b)
        # Then iterate b:
        for b in range(1,n+1):
            group = by_b[b]
            if not group: continue
            # compute max1, count_max1, max2 in group
            max1 = -1; count_max1=0; max2=-1
            for a, idx in group:
                if a > max1:
                    max2 = max1
                    max1 = a
                    count_max1 = 1
                elif a == max1:
                    count_max1 += 1
                elif a > max2:
                    max2 = a
            # But if max1 updated, max2 should be previous max1. If count_max1? Need handle duplicates of max. Let's compute more simply:
            # We can collect a values? group size total m, total O(m). Use loop:
            max1 = 0; max2 = 0; count_max1 = 0
            for a, idx in group:
                if a > max1:
                    max2 = max1
                    max1 = a
                    count_max1 = 1
                elif a == max1:
                    count_max1 += 1
                elif a > max2:
                    max2 = a
            # Is this correct if max1 appears multiple times and then new max? Suppose group a [5,5,3]. Loop:
            # a5 max1=5 count1 max2=0; a5 count2; a3 max2=3. max1=5 count2 max2=3. Good.
            # Suppose a [5,3,5]. After 5 max1=5; 3 max2=3; 5 count2. Good.
            # Suppose a [5,6,5]. 5 max1=5; 6>5 -> max2=5, max1=6, count1; 5 == max1? no, >max2? 5==max2 no. max1=6 count1 max2=5. Good.
            # Suppose a [5,6,6]. 5 max1=5;6 max2=5 max1=6 count1;6 count2. Good.
            # Suppose a [5,6,7]. 5 max1=5;6 max2=5 max1=6;7 max2=6 max1=7. Good.
            # Good.
            for a, idx in group:
                L_low[idx] = max_a_less
                if a == max1 and count_max1 == 1:
                    E_b[idx] = max2
                else:
                    E_b[idx] = max1
            # update max_a_less with max a in group
            if max1 > max_a_less:
                max_a_less = max1
        # Note L_low for group uses max_a_less before update. Good.

        # Compute M1, M2 arrays using Fenwick
        class Fenwick:
            def __init__(self,n):
                self.n=n
                self.bit=[0]*(n+1)
            def add(self,i,delta):
                n=self.n; bit=self.bit
                while i<=n:
                    bit[i]+=delta; i+=i&-i
            def kth(self,k):
                # smallest idx such that prefix sum >= k, assuming k>=1 and total>=k
                idx=0
                bitmask=1 << (self.n.bit_length()-1)
                bit=self.bit; n=self.n
                while bitmask:
                    nxt=idx+bitmask
                    if nxt<=n and bit[nxt] < k:
                        idx=nxt
                        k-=bit[nxt]
                    bitmask >>=1
                return idx+1
        fw=Fenwick(n)
        M1=[n+1]*(n+2)
        M2=[n+1]*(n+2)
        total_occurrences=0
        total_original=0
        for l in range(n,0,-1):
            for b in starts[l]:
                fw.add(b,1)
                total_occurrences +=1
            if total_occurrences >=1:
                M1[l] = fw.kth(1)
            else:
                M1[l] = n+1
            if total_occurrences >=2:
                M2[l] = fw.kth(2)
            else:
                M2[l] = n+1
            total_original += M1[l] - l
        # prefix M2
        pref=[0]*(n+1)
        s=0
        for i in range(1,n+1):
            s += M2[i]
            pref[i]=s
        max_gain=0
        for idx in range(m):
            a=occ_a[idx]; b=occ_b[idx]
            L = L_low[idx] if L_low[idx] >= E_b[idx] else E_b[idx]
            if L < a:
                length = a - L
                sum_m2 = pref[a] - pref[L]
                gain = sum_m2 - b * length
                if gain > max_gain:
                    max_gain = gain
        return total_original + max_gain

Need check Fenwick kth bitmask: self.n.bit_length()-1 gives highest power <=? For n=5, bit_length=3, 1<<2=4, okay. For n=1, bit_length=1, 1<<0=1. Good. But if n=0 no. Standard.

Potential issue: Fenwick kth(2) when total_occurrences>=2 but there may be count at b=?? kth returns second occurrence. Good.

Could optimize M1/M2 without Fenwick to avoid O(n log n) but okay. Need consider time in Python with m=2e5, n=1e5, Fenwick add for each occurrence (2e5*log) and kth for each l (1e5*2*log) ~ (4e5*17)=6.8M operations, fine. Actually add 2e5*17=3.4M, kth 2e5*17=3.4M, total ~7M, okay. Python fine.

But wait M2(l) as second smallest b among occurrences with a>=l. We compute after adding all starts at l. But for l where no starts, total_occurrences unchanged. Good.

Potential issue: If b values up to n, Fenwick size n. M1/M2 n+1 if none. Good.

But to be safe, we can implement Fenwick functions nested with local bit and n, and use `fw_add`, `fw_kth`. That might be faster than class. But class is fine. Let's decide. Final code should be clear. We can write:

bit = [0]*(n+1)
def add(i, delta):
    while i <= n:
        bit[i] += delta
        i += i & -i
def kth(k):
    idx=0
    step = 1 << (n.bit_length()-1)
    while step:
        nxt = idx + step
        if nxt <= n and bit[nxt] < k:
            idx = nxt
            k -= bit[nxt]
        step >>= 1
    return idx + 1

This avoids class. Need `n` captured. Good.

Then in loop:

    add(bval,1)
    total +=1
if total:
    M1[l]=kth(1)
else ...
if total >=2:
    M2[l]=kth(2)
else ...
